I guess I came in because I just feel… wound up, almost all day. It’s hard to explain exactly, but it’s like my body is constantly bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I can feel this tension in me, like I’m ready for some problem to pop up, even if I’m just doing ordinary things.

And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t really switch off. I’ll sit down and think, “Okay, now I can calm down,” but my body still feels tight and on edge. It’s not dramatic every minute, but it’s there enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.

Yes… I think that’s exactly what’s been bothering me. It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.

It’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.

So yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.

Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.

My mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.

So, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.

It’s not dramatic every minute, but it’s there enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of fear or worry that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what’s been bothering me. It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, just focus on this report,” because I work at the computer and I need to stay on task, but then one small thought turns into five more.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Did I miss a message?” and then I’m checking things, and then I’m replaying what could go wrong, or whether I should have responded differently, or what if something gets overlooked. And before I realize it, half an hour has gone by and I haven’t really done what I meant to do. It feels silly sometimes because I know I’m trying to reason with myself, but the worry doesn’t listen very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel uncomfortable and tense, but it doesn’t usually come on suddenly like an attack or something that peaks really fast. It’s more… drawn out, if that makes sense. Like a steady buildup or background hum rather than this sharp wave that takes over in a few minutes.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice I’m very keyed up, but when I think back, it’s usually because I’ve been worrying for a while already, not because it came out of nowhere all at once. So I don’t think I’ve had those sudden intense episodes you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.\n\nIt’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.

Like at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.

And the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.

It can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.

And a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.

could go wrong.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.\n  manifestation: I came in because I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when I’m just sitting at my computer or trying to relax after dinner.\n- key: S060\n  name: Irritability\n  description: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’m usually patient, but lately little things like a late update in a documentation meeting, someone singing off-tempo at choir, or too many questions while I’m cooking on Sunday make me answer sharper than I mean to.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still getting my work done and showing up for family and church, but it takes me longer to finish reports, I double-check things more than I used to, and I’ve had to apologize for sounding short with people I care about.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about thirteen months, since my sister in Arecibo started needing much more help from the family.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S058\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.\n\nIt can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. I’m still going to work, I’m still getting my reports done, I still show up for my family and for church when I’ve said I would. So from the outside, I think people might say I’m functioning fine.

But it takes me longer than it used to. A report that should be straightforward can take extra time because I keep going back and checking it again, or rereading an email before I send it, or making sure I didn’t miss some detail. And sometimes I know I’ve already checked something, but I’ll still feel like I need to look one more time, just to be sure.

And with people I care about, I’ve noticed I can sound short or impatient, especially when my mind is already full. I don’t mean to, but then I’ll hear my tone afterward and feel bad, and I’ve had to apologize. So yes, it interferes—not in a way where I’ve stopped doing my life, but it makes things heavier and slower, and it affects how I am with people.

never really rested.\n- key: S056\n  name: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\n  description: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.\n  manifestation: My mind keeps going from one concern to another—my sister in Arecibo, whether I missed something at the office, my husband driving home, church commitments, bills—and I find myself preparing for problems that haven’t even happened.\n- key: S057\n  name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: I’ll tell myself, ‘Daniela, enough, focus on this report,’ but one small thought turns into five more, and before I know it I’ve spent half an hour checking messages or replaying what could go wrong.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.\n  manifestation: I came in because I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when I’m just sitting at my computer or trying to relax after dinner.\n- key: S060\n  name: Irritability\n  description: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’m usually patient, but lately little things like a late update in a documentation meeting, someone singing off-tempo at choir, or too many questions while I’m cooking on Sunday make me answer sharper than I mean to.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still getting my work done and showing up for family and church, but it takes me longer to finish reports, I double-check things more than I used to, and I’ve had to apologize for sounding short with people I care about.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about thirteen months, since my sister in Arecibo started needing much more help from the family.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S058\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.\n\nIt can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. I’m still going to work, I’m still getting my reports done, I still show up for my family and for church when I’ve said I would. So from the outside, I think people might say I’m functioning fine.\n\nBut it takes me longer than it used to. A report that should be straightforward can take extra time because I keep going back and checking it again, or rereading an email before I send it, or making sure I didn’t miss some detail. And sometimes I know I’ve already checked something, but I’ll still feel like I need to look one more time, just to be sure.\n\nAnd with people I care about, I’ve noticed I can sound short or impatient, especially when my mind is already full. I don’t mean to, but then I’ll hear my tone afterward and feel bad, and I’ve had to apologize. So yes, it interferes—not in a way where I’ve stopped doing my life, but it makes things heavier and slower, and it affects how I am with people.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when there isn’t anything obvious happening.

It can be something completely ordinary, like I’m just sitting at my computer, not in a meeting, not dealing with a crisis, just doing regular work, and I still feel like I’m sort of tightened up inside. Like I’m waiting for something to go wrong or like I need to be ready to respond, even if there’s no real reason at that moment.

And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t always just turn off. I’ll sit down and think, okay, now I can rest, but my body doesn’t seem to get the message. I’m still tense, still on alert. It’s not dramatic, like I’m pacing the room all day, but there’s this constant wound-up feeling that’s hard to shake.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I just feel… wound up, almost all day. It’s hard to explain exactly, but it’s like my body is constantly bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I can feel this tension in me, like I’m ready for some problem to pop up, even if I’m just doing ordinary things.\n\nAnd then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t really switch off. I’ll sit down and think, “Okay, now I can calm down,” but my body still feels tight and on edge. It’s not dramatic every minute, but it’s there enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of fear or worry that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what’s been bothering me. It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, just focus on this report,” because I work at the computer and I need to stay on task, but then one small thought turns into five more.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Did I miss a message?” and then I’m checking things, and then I’m replaying what could go wrong, or whether I should have responded differently, or what if something gets overlooked. And before I realize it, half an hour has gone by and I haven’t really done what I meant to do. It feels silly sometimes because I know I’m trying to reason with myself, but the worry doesn’t listen very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel uncomfortable and tense, but it doesn’t usually come on suddenly like an attack or something that peaks really fast. It’s more… drawn out, if that makes sense. Like a steady buildup or background hum rather than this sharp wave that takes over in a few minutes.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice I’m very keyed up, but when I think back, it’s usually because I’ve been worrying for a while already, not because it came out of nowhere all at once. So I don’t think I’ve had those sudden intense episodes you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.\n\nIt’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.\n\nIt can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. A report that should be straightforward can take extra time because I keep going back and checking it again, or rereading an email before I send it, or making sure I didn’t miss some detail. And sometimes I know I’ve already checked something, but I’ll still feel like I need to look one more time, just to be sure.\n\nAnd with people I care about, I’ve noticed I can sound short or impatient, especially when my mind is already full. I don’t mean to, but then I’ll hear my tone afterward and feel bad, and I’ve had to apologize. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s distressing most days, but I’d describe it as… manageable, I guess, not completely overwhelming. I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. I get to work, I finish my reports, I show up for my family, and I still go to church and help when I said I would. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.

But inside, it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. A report that used to be pretty routine can take me much longer because I keep going back over it, checking details, rereading what I wrote, making sure I didn’t miss anything. And I know some of that sounds responsible, but it doesn’t feel calm or productive—it feels like I can’t quite trust that it’s okay.

And then with my family, or people close to me, I notice it comes out in my tone sometimes. I’ll sound short or impatient, and then I feel terrible afterward because they didn’t deserve that. I’ve had to apologize more than once, and that bothers me.

So daily distress… I’d say it’s there in the background a lot. I can get through the day, but it makes the day heavier, slower, and I don’t feel like myself as much as I’d like to.

It’s not dramatic every minute, but it’s there enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of fear or worry that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what’s been bothering me. It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, just focus on this report,” because I work at the computer and I need to stay on task, but then one small thought turns into five more.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Did I miss a message?” and then I’m checking things, and then I’m replaying what could go wrong, or whether I should have responded differently, or what if something gets overlooked. And before I realize it, half an hour has gone by and I haven’t really done what I meant to do. It feels silly sometimes because I know I’m trying to reason with myself, but the worry doesn’t listen very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel uncomfortable and tense, but it doesn’t usually come on suddenly like an attack or something that peaks really fast. It’s more… drawn out, if that makes sense. Like a steady buildup or background hum rather than this sharp wave that takes over in a few minutes.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice I’m very keyed up, but when I think back, it’s usually because I’ve been worrying for a while already, not because it came out of nowhere all at once. So I don’t think I’ve had those sudden intense episodes you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.\n\nIt’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. So I don’t think I’ve had those sudden intense episodes you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.\n\nIt’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.\n\nIt can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. I’m still going to work, I’m still getting my reports done, I still show up for my family and for church when I’ve said I would. So from the outside, I think people might say I’m functioning fine.\n\nBut it takes me longer than it used to. A report that should be straightforward can take extra time because I keep going back and checking it again, or rereading an email before I send it, or making sure I didn’t miss some detail. And sometimes I know I’ve already checked something, but I’ll still feel like I need to look one more time, just to be sure.\n\nAnd with people I care about, I’ve noticed I can sound short or impatient, especially when my mind is already full. I don’t mean to, but then I’ll hear my tone afterward and feel bad, and I’ve had to apologize. So yes, it interferes—not in a way where I’ve stopped doing my life, but it makes things heavier and slower, and it affects how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when there isn’t anything obvious happening.\n\nIt can be something completely ordinary, like I’m just sitting at my computer, not in a meeting, not dealing with a crisis, just doing regular work, and I still feel like I’m sort of tightened up inside. Like I’m waiting for something to go wrong or like I need to be ready to respond, even if there’s no real reason at that moment.\n\nAnd then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t always just turn off. I’ll sit down and think, okay, now I can rest, but my body doesn’t seem to get the message. I’m still tense, still on alert. It’s not dramatic, like I’m pacing the room all day, but there’s this constant wound-up feeling that’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel your worry and tension are causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing most days, but I’d describe it as… manageable, I guess, not completely overwhelming. I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. I get to work, I finish my reports, I show up for my family, and I still go to church and help when I said I would. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut inside, it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. A report that used to be pretty routine can take me much longer because I keep going back over it, checking details, rereading what I wrote, making sure I didn’t miss anything. And I know some of that sounds responsible, but it doesn’t feel calm or productive—it feels like I can’t quite trust that it’s okay.\n\nAnd then with my family, or people close to me, I notice it comes out in my tone sometimes. I’ll sound short or impatient, and then I feel terrible afterward because they didn’t deserve that. I’ve had to apologize more than once, and that bothers me.\n\nSo daily distress… I’d say it’s there in the background a lot. I can get through the day, but it makes the day heavier, slower, and I don’t feel like myself as much as I’d like to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about thirteen months now, if I’m counting it honestly.

It really seemed to start when my sister in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from the family. At first I thought, well, of course I’m worried, that’s normal, families go through things. But it didn’t really settle down after the first few weeks or months. It just kept going, and then the worrying became sort of… part of my daily rhythm, if that makes sense.

So yes, it’s been over a year now—around thirteen months.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It started with my sister, so at first I would have said it was mostly centered on her situation in Arecibo. That was the thing I could point to and say, “This is why I’m worried.”

But honestly, now it jumps around. It’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one track. I’ll be thinking about my sister and whether she needs something, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work—like, did I miss something in a report, did I send the wrong version, should I double-check that email again. Then it might shift to my husband driving home, and I’ll start imagining, what if something happens on the road, what if he doesn’t call when he said he would.

And then there are smaller things too, like church commitments, whether I promised to help with something and forgot a detail, or bills and whether I overlooked a payment. It’s not always one big crisis. Sometimes it’s just this chain of “what ifs,” one after another.

I find myself preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet—like mentally rehearsing what I would do if something went wrong. So no, it’s not just my sister anymore. She’s still part of it, definitely, but the worry spreads into a lot of different areas.

It’s hard to explain exactly, but it’s like my body is constantly bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I can feel this tension in me, like I’m ready for some problem to pop up, even if I’m just doing ordinary things.\n\nAnd then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t really switch off. I’ll sit down and think, “Okay, now I can calm down,” but my body still feels tight and on edge. It’s not dramatic every minute, but it’s there enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of fear or worry that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what’s been bothering me. It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, just focus on this report,” because I work at the computer and I need to stay on task, but then one small thought turns into five more.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Did I miss a message?” and then I’m checking things, and then I’m replaying what could go wrong, or whether I should have responded differently, or what if something gets overlooked. And before I realize it, half an hour has gone by and I haven’t really done what I meant to do. It feels silly sometimes because I know I’m trying to reason with myself, but the worry doesn’t listen very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel uncomfortable and tense, but it doesn’t usually come on suddenly like an attack or something that peaks really fast. It’s more… drawn out, if that makes sense. Like a steady buildup or background hum rather than this sharp wave that takes over in a few minutes.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice I’m very keyed up, but when I think back, it’s usually because I’ve been worrying for a while already, not because it came out of nowhere all at once. So I don’t think I’ve had those sudden intense episodes you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.\n\nIt’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.\n\nIt can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. I’m still going to work, I’m still getting my reports done, I still show up for my family and for church when I’ve said I would. So from the outside, I think people might say I’m functioning fine.\n\nBut it takes me longer than it used to. A report that should be straightforward can take extra time because I keep going back and checking it again, or rereading an email before I send it, or making sure I didn’t miss some detail. And sometimes I know I’ve already checked something, but I’ll still feel like I need to look one more time, just to be sure.\n\nAnd with people I care about, I’ve noticed I can sound short or impatient, especially when my mind is already full. I don’t mean to, but then I’ll hear my tone afterward and feel bad, and I’ve had to apologize. So yes, it interferes—not in a way where I’ve stopped doing my life, but it makes things heavier and slower, and it affects how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when there isn’t anything obvious happening.\n\nIt can be something completely ordinary, like I’m just sitting at my computer, not in a meeting, not dealing with a crisis, just doing regular work, and I still feel like I’m sort of tightened up inside. Like I’m waiting for something to go wrong or like I need to be ready to respond, even if there’s no real reason at that moment.\n\nAnd then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t always just turn off. I’ll sit down and think, okay, now I can rest, but my body doesn’t seem to get the message. I’m still tense, still on alert. It’s not dramatic, like I’m pacing the room all day, but there’s this constant wound-up feeling that’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel your worry and tension are causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing most days, but I’d describe it as… manageable, I guess, not completely overwhelming. I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. I get to work, I finish my reports, I show up for my family, and I still go to church and help when I said I would. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut inside, it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. A report that used to be pretty routine can take me much longer because I keep going back over it, checking details, rereading what I wrote, making sure I didn’t miss anything. And I know some of that sounds responsible, but it doesn’t feel calm or productive—it feels like I can’t quite trust that it’s okay.\n\nAnd then with my family, or people close to me, I notice it comes out in my tone sometimes. I’ll sound short or impatient, and then I feel terrible afterward because they didn’t deserve that. I’ve had to apologize more than once, and that bothers me.\n\nSo daily distress… I’d say it’s there in the background a lot. I can get through the day, but it makes the day heavier, slower, and I don’t feel like myself as much as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been worrying excessively for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about thirteen months now, if I’m counting it honestly.\n\nIt really seemed to start when my sister in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from the family. At first I thought, well, of course I’m worried, that’s normal, families go through things. But it didn’t really settle down after the first few weeks or months. It just kept going, and then the worrying became sort of… part of my daily rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it’s been over a year now—around thirteen months.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually centered around one main issue, such as your sister's situation, or does it cover a wide range of topics and concerns?\n\nPatient: It started with my sister, so at first I would have said it was mostly centered on her situation in Arecibo. That was the thing I could point to and say, “This is why I’m worried.”\n\nBut honestly, now it jumps around. It’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one track. I’ll be thinking about my sister and whether she needs something, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work—like, did I miss something in a report, did I send the wrong version, should I double-check that email again. Then it might shift to my husband driving home, and I’ll start imagining, what if something happens on the road, what if he doesn’t call when he said he would.\n\nAnd then there are smaller things too, like church commitments, whether I promised to help with something and forgot a detail, or bills and whether I overlooked a payment. It’s not always one big crisis. Sometimes it’s just this chain of “what ifs,” one after another.\n\nI find myself preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet—like mentally rehearsing what I would do if something went wrong. So no, it’s not just my sister anymore. She’s still part of it, definitely, but the worry spreads into a lot of different areas.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Sometimes it’s just this chain of “what ifs,” one after another.\n\nI find myself preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet—like mentally rehearsing what I would do if something went wrong. So no, it’s not just my sister anymore. She’s still part of it, definitely, but the worry spreads into a lot of different areas.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often notice that your worry is accompanied by physical sensations like muscle tension or tightness?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel your worry and tension are causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing most days, but I’d describe it as… manageable, I guess, not completely overwhelming. I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. I get to work, I finish my reports, I show up for my family, and I still go to church and help when I said I would. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut inside, it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. A report that used to be pretty routine can take me much longer because I keep going back over it, checking details, rereading what I wrote, making sure I didn’t miss anything. And I know some of that sounds responsible, but it doesn’t feel calm or productive—it feels like I can’t quite trust that it’s okay.\n\nAnd then with my family, or people close to me, I notice it comes out in my tone sometimes. I’ll sound short or impatient, and then I feel terrible afterward because they didn’t deserve that. I’ve had to apologize more than once, and that bothers me.\n\nSo daily distress… I’d say it’s there in the background a lot. I can get through the day, but it makes the day heavier, slower, and I don’t feel like myself as much as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been worrying excessively for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about thirteen months now, if I’m counting it honestly.\n\nIt really seemed to start when my sister in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from the family. At first I thought, well, of course I’m worried, that’s normal, families go through things. But it didn’t really settle down after the first few weeks or months. It just kept going, and then the worrying became sort of… part of my daily rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it’s been over a year now—around thirteen months.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually centered around one main issue, such as your sister's situation, or does it cover a wide range of topics and concerns?\n\nPatient: It started with my sister, so at first I would have said it was mostly centered on her situation in Arecibo. That was the thing I could point to and say, “This is why I’m worried.”\n\nBut honestly, now it jumps around. It’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one track. I’ll be thinking about my sister and whether she needs something, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work—like, did I miss something in a report, did I send the wrong version, should I double-check that email again. Then it might shift to my husband driving home, and I’ll start imagining, what if something happens on the road, what if he doesn’t call when he said he would.\n\nAnd then there are smaller things too, like church commitments, whether I promised to help with something and forgot a detail, or bills and whether I overlooked a payment. It’s not always one big crisis. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when there isn’t anything happening right then.

It can be when I’m just sitting at my computer, doing something ordinary, and I’ll notice I’m holding myself really tight—like my shoulders or my whole posture is just… guarded. And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it’s still there. I’ll be sitting down, trying to unwind, and my body doesn’t seem to get the message that everything is okay.

It’s not always dramatic, but it’s persistent. Like I’m physically on alert most of the day.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I just feel… wound up, almost all day. It’s hard to explain exactly, but it’s like my body is constantly bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I can feel this tension in me, like I’m ready for some problem to pop up, even if I’m just doing ordinary things.\n\nAnd then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t really switch off. I’ll sit down and think, “Okay, now I can calm down,” but my body still feels tight and on edge. It’s not dramatic every minute, but it’s there enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of fear or worry that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what’s been bothering me. It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, just focus on this report,” because I work at the computer and I need to stay on task, but then one small thought turns into five more.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Did I miss a message?” and then I’m checking things, and then I’m replaying what could go wrong, or whether I should have responded differently, or what if something gets overlooked. And before I realize it, half an hour has gone by and I haven’t really done what I meant to do. It feels silly sometimes because I know I’m trying to reason with myself, but the worry doesn’t listen very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel uncomfortable and tense, but it doesn’t usually come on suddenly like an attack or something that peaks really fast. It’s more… drawn out, if that makes sense. Like a steady buildup or background hum rather than this sharp wave that takes over in a few minutes.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice I’m very keyed up, but when I think back, it’s usually because I’ve been worrying for a while already, not because it came out of nowhere all at once. So I don’t think I’ve had those sudden intense episodes you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive in the way people sometimes mean, like a strange thought popping in out of nowhere, but yes, I do get thoughts I don’t want that sort of barge in and then won’t leave me alone.\n\nIt’s usually worry thoughts. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes. I mean, I can usually see afterward that I was jumping ahead too far, or preparing for problems that hadn’t even happened. But in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind keeps moving from one thing to another. I’ll start with something small, like, “Did I miss something at the office?” and then it becomes, “What if someone is waiting on me?” or “What if I forgot a detail?” Then it shifts to my sister in Arecibo, wondering if she’s okay, or my husband driving home, or whether I’m keeping up with church commitments, or bills coming due. It’s like my brain makes a whole list, and I feel like I have to mentally prepare for each possible thing going wrong.\n\nSo, rationally, I know not all of that is realistic at the same time. But it still feels hard to dismiss, because there’s always some little piece of it that could be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute of the day, but on most days, once the worrying starts, it’s really hard for me to pull myself back from it.\n\nLike at work, I’ll be trying to finish something simple, like a report, and I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I’m losing time. But then one small thought comes in, like maybe I missed an email or maybe there’s something I forgot to check, and then that turns into five more thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading what I already read, thinking through what could go wrong, and before I realize it, half an hour is gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know I’m doing it. It’s not like I’m unaware. I can see myself getting pulled into it, but it still feels very hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about a specific area of your life, like work or relationships, or does it seem to spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish it stayed in one box, like just work, because then maybe I could handle it more neatly, but it doesn’t.\n\nIt can start with work, like thinking I may have missed something at the office or didn’t answer someone quickly enough, and then before I know it my mind jumps to my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, then to my husband driving home, then to church commitments I said I would help with, then bills or something around the house. It’s like one concern opens the door to the next one.\n\nAnd a lot of the time I’m not even dealing with an actual problem in front of me. I’m preparing in my head for things that haven’t happened yet—what if this goes wrong, what if I forgot that, what if someone needs me and I’m not ready. So no, it’s not just one area. It moves around.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that. I’m still going to work, I’m still getting my reports done, I still show up for my family and for church when I’ve said I would. So from the outside, I think people might say I’m functioning fine.\n\nBut it takes me longer than it used to. A report that should be straightforward can take extra time because I keep going back and checking it again, or rereading an email before I send it, or making sure I didn’t miss some detail. And sometimes I know I’ve already checked something, but I’ll still feel like I need to look one more time, just to be sure.\n\nAnd with people I care about, I’ve noticed I can sound short or impatient, especially when my mind is already full. I don’t mean to, but then I’ll hear my tone afterward and feel bad, and I’ve had to apologize. So yes, it interferes—not in a way where I’ve stopped doing my life, but it makes things heavier and slower, and it affects how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when there isn’t anything obvious happening.\n\nIt can be something completely ordinary, like I’m just sitting at my computer, not in a meeting, not dealing with a crisis, just doing regular work, and I still feel like I’m sort of tightened up inside. Like I’m waiting for something to go wrong or like I need to be ready to respond, even if there’s no real reason at that moment.\n\nAnd then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it doesn’t always just turn off. I’ll sit down and think, okay, now I can rest, but my body doesn’t seem to get the message. I’m still tense, still on alert. It’s not dramatic, like I’m pacing the room all day, but there’s this constant wound-up feeling that’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel your worry and tension are causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing most days, but I’d describe it as… manageable, I guess, not completely overwhelming. I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. I get to work, I finish my reports, I show up for my family, and I still go to church and help when I said I would. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut inside, it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. A report that used to be pretty routine can take me much longer because I keep going back over it, checking details, rereading what I wrote, making sure I didn’t miss anything. And I know some of that sounds responsible, but it doesn’t feel calm or productive—it feels like I can’t quite trust that it’s okay.\n\nAnd then with my family, or people close to me, I notice it comes out in my tone sometimes. I’ll sound short or impatient, and then I feel terrible afterward because they didn’t deserve that. I’ve had to apologize more than once, and that bothers me.\n\nSo daily distress… I’d say it’s there in the background a lot. I can get through the day, but it makes the day heavier, slower, and I don’t feel like myself as much as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been worrying excessively for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about thirteen months now, if I’m counting it honestly.\n\nIt really seemed to start when my sister in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from the family. At first I thought, well, of course I’m worried, that’s normal, families go through things. But it didn’t really settle down after the first few weeks or months. It just kept going, and then the worrying became sort of… part of my daily rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it’s been over a year now—around thirteen months.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually centered around one main issue, such as your sister's situation, or does it cover a wide range of topics and concerns?\n\nPatient: It started with my sister, so at first I would have said it was mostly centered on her situation in Arecibo. Sometimes it’s just this chain of “what ifs,” one after another.\n\nI find myself preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet—like mentally rehearsing what I would do if something went wrong. So no, it’s not just my sister anymore. I’ll sit down and think, okay, now I can rest, but my body doesn’t seem to get the message. I’m still tense, still on alert. It’s not dramatic, like I’m pacing the room all day, but there’s this constant wound-up feeling that’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel your worry and tension are causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing most days, but I’d describe it as… manageable, I guess, not completely overwhelming. I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. I get to work, I finish my reports, I show up for my family, and I still go to church and help when I said I would. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut inside, it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. A report that used to be pretty routine can take me much longer because I keep going back over it, checking details, rereading what I wrote, making sure I didn’t miss anything. And I know some of that sounds responsible, but it doesn’t feel calm or productive—it feels like I can’t quite trust that it’s okay.\n\nAnd then with my family, or people close to me, I notice it comes out in my tone sometimes. I’ll sound short or impatient, and then I feel terrible afterward because they didn’t deserve that. I’ve had to apologize more than once, and that bothers me.\n\nSo daily distress… I’d say it’s there in the background a lot. I can get through the day, but it makes the day heavier, slower, and I don’t feel like myself as much as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been worrying excessively for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about thirteen months now, if I’m counting it honestly.\n\nIt really seemed to start when my sister in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from the family. At first I thought, well, of course I’m worried, that’s normal, families go through things. But it didn’t really settle down after the first few weeks or months. It just kept going, and then the worrying became sort of… part of my daily rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it’s been over a year now—around thirteen months.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually centered around one main issue, such as your sister's situation, or does it cover a wide range of topics and concerns?\n\nPatient: It started with my sister, so at first I would have said it was mostly centered on her situation in Arecibo. That was the thing I could point to and say, “This is why I’m worried.”\n\nBut honestly, now it jumps around. It’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one track. I’ll be thinking about my sister and whether she needs something, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work—like, did I miss something in a report, did I send the wrong version, should I double-check that email again. Then it might shift to my husband driving home, and I’ll start imagining, what if something happens on the road, what if he doesn’t call when he said he would.\n\nAnd then there are smaller things too, like church commitments, whether I promised to help with something and forgot a detail, or bills and whether I overlooked a payment. It’s not always one big crisis. Sometimes it’s just this chain of “what ifs,” one after another.\n\nI find myself preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet—like mentally rehearsing what I would do if something went wrong. So no, it’s not just my sister anymore. She’s still part of it, definitely, but the worry spreads into a lot of different areas.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice that your worry is accompanied by physical sensations like muscle tension or tightness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is bracing for something, even when there isn’t anything happening right then.\n\nIt can be when I’m just sitting at my computer, doing something ordinary, and I’ll notice I’m holding myself really tight—like my shoulders or my whole posture is just… guarded. And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it’s still there. I’ll be sitting down, trying to unwind, and my body doesn’t seem to get the message that everything is okay.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s persistent. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t lost my job or stopped talking to people or anything like that. I’m still functioning, I guess is the word. I show up to work, I get my reports done, I go to church, I’m there for my family.

But it does affect things. At work, reports take me longer than they used to because I’ll keep going back over them. I’ll check a number, then check it again, then wonder if I checked the right version, and before I know it I’ve spent way more time on something that should have been straightforward. I don’t think my work quality has fallen apart, but it takes more out of me, and it’s definitely slower.

With relationships, it’s more that I get tense and then I sound short. Like my husband or someone in the family will ask me something normal, and I’ll answer in this clipped tone because my mind is already full of five other worries. Then afterward I feel bad and I’ll have to apologize, because they didn’t deserve that. Same with people I care about at church sometimes—not that I’m yelling or anything, but I can come across impatient.

So… yes, there are problems, but I’d call them mild. I’m still doing what I need to do, but it feels harder, and I can tell it spills over onto other people sometimes.

It’s not like one huge fear all the time, it’s more that once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it down. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, just focus on this report,” because I work at the computer and I need to stay on task, but then one small thought turns into five more.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Did I miss a message?” and then I’m checking things, and then I’m replaying what could go wrong, or whether I should have responded differently, or what if something gets overlooked. Like a steady buildup or background hum rather than this sharp wave that takes over in a few minutes.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice I’m very keyed up, but when I think back, it’s usually because I’ve been worrying for a while already, not because it came out of nowhere all at once. I’ll be trying to work on a report, and I’ll literally tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” because I know I need to get it done. But then one small thing starts it—like wondering if I missed a message, or if something could go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five other thoughts. Then I’m checking messages, rereading things, replaying what I said or what might happen, and before I know it, half an hour has gone by.\n\nSo yes, they feel unwanted, and I don’t feel like I can just shut them off when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worry thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Some of them, yes.
